\phantomsection\label{tab:coefficient-notation}
We use \(\theta\) for the half-angle Cayley coordinate \(y=\tan\theta\) and
\(z=e^{2\ii\theta}\) for the Laurent phase.  Thus
\(\dd\omega(y)=\dd y/(\pi(1+y^2))=\dd\theta/\pi\), and
\(\cos(2k\theta)\), \(\sin(2k\theta)\) become \(z^{\pm k}\) modes.  The
notation guide at the end of the Cayley--Haar preliminaries separates the
coefficient, mode, covariance, and dissipation symbols used below.  Locally, the
Laurent representative of \(u_n\) is denoted \(Q_n\), so
\(Q_n(e^{2\ii\theta})=u_n(\tan\theta)\).  Mode integrals are written
\(J(n_1,\ldots,n_r)\); the dissipation is always
\(\mathcal I_\nu(t)\).  The one-dimensional universal coefficient polynomial
is \(A_k^{\rm form}\), its evaluation on \(\nu\) is \(A_k(\nu)\), and
multidimensional retained coefficients are denoted
\(\mathcal A_m^{(d)}(\nu)\).  The separate symbol
\(\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)\) refers only to the algebraic covariance quadratic layer,
which equals \(\mathcal A_4^{(d)}(\nu)\) after a quotient jet has identified
the KL expansion.

\begin{theorem}[Cayley--Chebyshev mode formula]%
\label{thm:cayley-chebyshev-mode}
\label{lem:mode-parity-vanish}
Let \(U_n\) be the Chebyshev polynomial of the second kind.  If
\(y=\tan\theta\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cayley-chebyshev-mode}
u_n(\tan\theta)=\cos^n\theta\,U_n(\sin\theta).
\end{equation}
Equivalently, for \(n\ge1\),
\[
u_n(\tan\theta)=
\cos^{n-1}\theta\,
\sin\!\left((n+1)\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\theta\right)\right),
\]
and therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cayley-mode-parity}
u_n(-y)=(-1)^nu_n(y).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
\relocatedproof{sec_entropy_core_main.tex}{the proof following Theorem
``Cayley--Chebyshev mode formula''}
\else
\begin{proof}
With \(y=\tan\theta\),
\[
R_\varepsilon(\tan\theta)
=\{1-2(\varepsilon\cos\theta)\sin\theta
+(\varepsilon\cos\theta)^2\}^{-1}.
\]
The generating function
\(\sum_{n\ge0}U_n(x)r^n=(1-2xr+r^2)^{-1}\)
\cite[Chapters~1 and~5]{MasonHandscomb2002} gives
\eqref{eq:cayley-chebyshev-mode}.  The trigonometric form follows from the
standard identity for \(U_n(\sin\theta)\), and parity is immediate.
\end{proof}
\fi

For \(r\ge2\) and \(n_j\ge2\), define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mode-integral-definition}
J(n_1,\ldots,n_r):=
\int_{\RR}u_{n_1}(y)\cdots u_{n_r}(y)\,\omega(\dd y).
\end{equation}
In the formal polynomial algebra
\(\QQ[\mathfrak m_2,\ldots,\mathfrak m_k]\), put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient}
A_k^{\rm form}:=
\sum_{r=2}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}
\frac{(-1)^r}{r(r-1)}
\sum_{\substack{n_1+\cdots+n_r=k\\ n_j\ge2}}
\mathfrak m_{n_1}\cdots\mathfrak m_{n_r}\,J(n_1,\ldots,n_r).
\end{equation}
When the required centred moments exist,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient-evaluation}
A_k(\nu):=\operatorname{ev}_{\nu,k}(A_k^{\rm form}),
\qquad
\operatorname{ev}_{\nu,k}(\mathfrak m_n)=\mu_n .
\end{equation}

\begin{definition}[Canonical coefficient finiteness]
\label{def:canonical-coefficient-finiteness}
Let \(k\ge4\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR\) with finite
mean.  We say that \(A_k(\nu)\) is \emph{finite}, and write
\(A_k(\nu)\in\RR\), when the reduced polynomial
\(A_k^{\rm form}\) admits its canonical Lebesgue evaluation at the centred
moments of \(\nu\): every moment occurring in a nonzero monomial is an
absolutely integrable, finite real number, and ordinary real arithmetic then
gives \eqref{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient-evaluation}.  Otherwise we
write \(A_k(\nu)\notin\RR\).  In particular, no value is assigned by a
principal value, by a choice of truncation, or by cancellation of divergent
quantities.  This is a deliberately specified domain for evaluation of the
universal coefficient polynomial.  It is not defined through the existence
of an entropy remainder.  Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-even-orders} separately
identifies the evaluated polynomial with an entropy-asymptotic coefficient
under its finite-moment hypothesis.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Universal entropy coefficients]%
\label{thm:universal-entropy-coefficients}
The coefficient of \(t^{-k}\) in the formal expression
\[
\int_{\RR}\left\{
\left(1+\sum_{n=2}^{k}\mathfrak m_nu_n(y)t^{-n}\right)
\log\left(1+\sum_{n=2}^{k}\mathfrak m_nu_n(y)t^{-n}\right)
-\sum_{n=2}^{k}\mathfrak m_nu_n(y)t^{-n}
\right\}\omega(\dd y)
\]
is \(A_k^{\rm form}\).  If the moments needed in
\eqref{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient} exist for \(\nu\), then
\[
A_k(\nu)=
\sum_{r=2}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}
\frac{(-1)^r}{r(r-1)}
\sum_{\substack{n_1+\cdots+n_r=k\\ n_j\ge2}}
\mu_{n_1}\cdots\mu_{n_r}\,J(n_1,\ldots,n_r).
\]
All odd formal coefficients vanish.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use
\[
(1+s)\log(1+s)-s=\sum_{r=2}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^r}{r(r-1)}s^r.
\]
The coefficient of total degree \(k\) is finite and is exactly
\eqref{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient}.  If \(k\) is odd, then each mode
product in the coefficient has odd total parity by
\eqref{eq:cayley-mode-parity}, hence integrates to zero against the even
measure \(\omega\).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite Fourier algorithm for mode integrals]%
\label{thm:finite-fourier-mode-integral-algorithm}
Define \(Q_n(e^{2\ii\theta})=u_n(\tan\theta)\).  Equivalently, for every
\(n\ge1\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mode-laurent-polynomial-definition}
Q_n(z)=2^{-n}(-\ii)^n
\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{n-1}{k-1}
\bigl(z^k+(-1)^nz^{-k}\bigr).
\end{equation}
Thus, if \(Q_n(z)=\sum_kq_{n,k}z^k\), then \(q_{n,0}=0\) and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mode-laurent-coefficients}
q_{n,k}=
2^{-n}(-\ii)^n\operatorname{sgn}(k)^{\,n\bmod2}
\binom{n-1}{|k|-1},
\qquad 0<|k|\le n,
\end{equation}
with \(q_{n,k}=0\) for \(|k|>n\).
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mode-integral-constant-term}
J(n_1,\ldots,n_r)=[z^0]\prod_{j=1}^r Q_{n_j}(z).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
\relocatedproof{sec_entropy_core_main.tex}{the proof following Theorem
``Finite Fourier algorithm for mode integrals''}
\else
\begin{proof}
Put \(w=e^{\ii\theta}\) and \(z=w^2\).  The trigonometric form in
Theorem~\ref{thm:cayley-chebyshev-mode} gives
\[
u_n(\tan\theta)
=\frac{\cos^{n-1}\theta}{2\ii}
\left\{\ii^{\,n+1}w^{-(n+1)}
-(-\ii)^{n+1}w^{n+1}\right\}.
\]
Since
\(\cos^{n-1}\theta=2^{-(n-1)}w^{-(n-1)}(1+w^2)^{n-1}\), this becomes
\[
2^{-n}(1+z)^{n-1}
\left\{\ii^nz^{-n}+(-\ii)^nz\right\}.
\]
Expanding \((1+z)^{n-1}\) and reindexing the negative powers gives
\eqref{eq:mode-laurent-polynomial-definition}; the coefficient formula
\eqref{eq:mode-laurent-coefficients} is the same identity read termwise.
Since \(\omega(\dd y)=\dd\theta/\pi\), the map \(z=e^{2\ii\theta}\) sends
the integral to Haar integration on \(\TT\), hence to constant-term
extraction.
\end{proof}
\fi

\begin{proposition}[Minimal absolute moment for universal polynomial evaluation]%
\label{thm:sharp-moment-equivalence-hierarchy}
Let \(m\ge2\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR\) with finite
mean \(\bar\gamma\).  Put \(X_c=\gamma-\bar\gamma\).  No variance or tail
assumption is imposed.  For \(m\ge3\), the reduced universal polynomial has
the form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sharp-moment-equivalence-top-layer}
A_{2m}^{\rm form}
=\kappa_m\,\mathfrak m_2\mathfrak m_{2m-2}
 +P_m(\mathfrak m_2,\ldots,\mathfrak m_{2m-3}),
\qquad
\kappa_m=(-1)^m(m-1)2^{-2m+2}\ne0,
\end{equation}
At \(m=2\), \(A_4^{\rm form}=\mathfrak m_2^2/8\).  Thus the exact top absolute moment
is sufficient for evaluating every monomial and is minimal among
absolute-moment assumptions that guarantee evaluation uniformly over the full
class of centred laws.  Equivalently, within the specially stipulated
canonical domain of Definition~\ref{def:canonical-coefficient-finiteness},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sharp-moment-equivalence-hierarchy}
 A_{2m}(\nu)\in\RR
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \EE|X_c|^{2m-2}<\infty .
\end{equation}
This equivalence describes only the domain of polynomial evaluation.  For
\(m\ge3\) it is not an unconditional converse for an entropy remainder.  When the
finite-moment condition holds, Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-even-orders}, with
\(N=m\), identifies this finite number as the actual \(t^{-2m}\)
coefficient in the entropy expansion under precisely the same finite
\((2m-2)\)nd absolute-moment hypothesis.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
First suppose that \(\EE|X_c|^{2m-2}<\infty\).  Lyapunov's inequality gives
\(\EE|X_c|^j<\infty\) for every \(0\le j\le2m-2\).  Every index in
\eqref{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient} is at most \(2m-2\), because every
entropy monomial has at least two factors and every index is at least two.
The coefficient therefore has a finite canonical evaluation.  This proves
the moment-to-coefficient implication without any additional tail
hypothesis.

For the converse and sharpness, first take \(m=2\).  The only composition
of four into parts at least two is \((2,2)\), and
\[
 A_4^{\rm form}=\frac12\mathfrak m_2^2J(2,2)
 =\frac18\mathfrak m_2^2,
\]
because \(J(2,2)=1/4\).  Hence its canonical evaluation is finite exactly
when \(\EE X_c^2<\infty\).

Let \(m\ge3\) and write \(L=2m-2\).  A monomial in
\eqref{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient} which contains
\(\mathfrak m_L\) and has total index \(2m=L+2\) must have exactly two
factors, of indices \((2,L)\) or \((L,2)\).  Thus
\[
 A_{2m}^{\rm form}
 =J(2,L)\mathfrak m_2\mathfrak m_L
  +P_m(\mathfrak m_2,\ldots,\mathfrak m_{L-1}).
\]
It remains to show that the displayed top variable genuinely occurs.  Put
\(L=2q\), so \(q=m-1\).  The Laurent formula
\eqref{eq:mode-laurent-polynomial-definition} gives
\[
Q_2(z)=-\frac14(z+z^{-1}+z^2+z^{-2})
\]
and
\[
Q_{2q}(z)=(-1)^q2^{-2q}
\sum_{j=0}^{2q-1}\binom{2q-1}{j}
\bigl(z^{j+1}+z^{-(j+1)}\bigr).
\]
Only the exponents \(\pm1,\pm2\) of \(Q_{2q}\) contribute to the constant
term of \(Q_2Q_{2q}\).  Therefore
\begin{align*}
J(2,2q)
&=[z^0]Q_2Q_{2q}\\
&=-\frac12(-1)^q2^{-2q}
  \left\{\binom{2q-1}{0}+\binom{2q-1}{1}\right\}\\
&=(-1)^{q+1}q2^{-2q}
 =(-1)^m(m-1)2^{-2m+2}=\kappa_m\ne0.
\end{align*}
This proves \eqref{eq:sharp-moment-equivalence-top-layer}.

Now assume that \(A_{2m}(\nu)\in\RR\).  Canonical finiteness already
requires every moment occurring in a nonzero monomial of the reduced
polynomial to be a finite Lebesgue moment.  The calculation above shows that
\(\mathfrak m_L\) is such a variable whenever \(0<\mu_2<\infty\), and hence
\(\mu_L=\EE|X_c|^L<\infty\), since \(L\) is even.  If \(\mu_2=0\), then
\(X_c=0\) almost surely and the same conclusion is automatic.  Infinite
variance is excluded already by canonical evaluability of the displayed
monomial.  Hence finite evaluation implies \(\EE|X_c|^{2m-2}<\infty\),
which completes the equivalence.

Finally, lower moments cannot replace the top moment.  For example, with
\(a_j=\mathrm e^j\) and sufficiently small \(c>0\), the centred symmetric
law
\[
\left(1-\sum_{j\ge1}\frac{c}{j a_j^L}\right)\delta_0
+\sum_{j\ge1}\frac{c}{2j a_j^L}
 (\delta_{a_j}+\delta_{-a_j})

 succeeded in 194ms:
\]
Thus the Taylor polynomial may be evaluated on the finite density polynomial
\(d_t\) through order \(L+2\), which proves the claim.
\end{proof}
\fi

\medskip
\phantomsection\label{par:coefficient-evaluability-scope}
\noindent\textbf{Finite-moment coefficient level.}
The finite-moment level for the hierarchy is summarized in
Subsection~\ref{sec:entropy-theorem-map}; the algebraic obstruction
behind the leading threshold is proved below in
Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-kl-moment-threshold-sharpness}.
\medskip

\begin{theorem}[Finite-moment Poisson entropy transfer and coefficient formula]%
\label{thm:poisson-kl-even-orders}
Let \(N\ge2\), put \(L=2N-2\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on
\(\RR\) with finite mean \(\bar{\gamma}\) and finite centred \(L\)th absolute
moment.  Let
\[
\begin{aligned}
X_c&=\gamma-\bar{\gamma},\\
\mu_n&=\int_{\RR} X_c^n\,\dd\nu(\gamma)\qquad(2\le n\le L),\\
h_t&=P_t*\nu,\qquad
g_t(x)&=P_t(x-\bar{\gamma}),
\end{aligned}
\]
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-kl-even-orders}
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)
=\sum_{m=2}^{N}A_{2m}(\nu)t^{-2m}+o(t^{-2N}).
\end{equation}
For \(4\le k\le2N\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coefficient-identification-transfer-expansion}
A_k(\nu)=
\sum_{r=2}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}
\frac{(-1)^r}{r(r-1)}
\sum_{\substack{n_1+\cdots+n_r=k\\2\le n_j\le2N-2}}
\mu_{n_1}\cdots\mu_{n_r}
\int_{\RR}u_{n_1}\cdots u_{n_r}\,\dd\omega .
\end{equation}
All retained odd \(A_k\) vanish.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write
\[
d_t(y)=\sum_{n=2}^{L}\mu_nu_n(y)t^{-n}.
\]
Lemma~\ref{lem:exact-moment-density-truncation} gives
\(\delta_t=d_t+\rho_t\),
\(\|\rho_t\|_\infty+\|\rho_t\|_{L^1(\omega)}=o(t^{-L})\).  The Cayley modes
\(u_2,\ldots,u_L\) are bounded, hence \(\|d_t\|_\infty=O(t^{-2})\).  Since
\[
g_t(\bar{\gamma}+ty)t\,\dd y=\omega(\dd y),
\]
and \(\int\delta_t\,\dd\omega=0\),
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)
=\int_{\RR}\Phi(\delta_t(y))\,\omega(\dd y),
\qquad
\Phi(s)=(1+s)\log(1+s)-s .
\]
Applying
Proposition~\ref{prop:finite-density-jet-entropy-transfer} with
\((E,\lambda)=(\RR,\omega)\) gives
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)
=
\sum_{r=2}^{N}\frac{(-1)^r}{r(r-1)}
\int_{\RR}\left(\sum_{n=2}^{L}\mu_nu_n(y)t^{-n}\right)^r\omega(\dd y)
+o(t^{-2N}).
\]
Extracting the Laurent coefficient of \(t^{-k}\) gives
\eqref{eq:coefficient-identification-transfer-expansion}, and the odd
orders vanish by Theorem~\ref{thm:universal-entropy-coefficients}.

The constant-term evaluation uses a single Laurent convention throughout:
\[
Q_n(z)=2^{-n}(-\ii)^n
\sum_{k=1}^{n}\binom{n-1}{k-1}
\bigl(z^k+(-1)^nz^{-k}\bigr),
\qquad
J(n_1,\ldots,n_r)=[z^0]\prod_{j=1}^r Q_{n_j}(z).
\]
Equivalently, \(Q_n(z)=\sum_kq_{n,k}z^k\), \(q_{n,0}=0\), and
\[
q_{n,k}=2^{-n}(-\ii)^n
\operatorname{sgn}(k)^{\,n\bmod2}
\binom{n-1}{|k|-1}\qquad(0<|k|\le n).
\]
Thus the signs of the negative odd modes are already contained in
\(q_{n,k}\).  For example, with \(\sigma^2=\mu_2\),
\[
A_4=\frac{\sigma^4}{8},\qquad
A_6=\frac{\sigma^6+6\mu_3^2-8\sigma^2\mu_4}{64};
\]
  the sixth-order arithmetic is displayed in
  \path{sec_entropy_n4_defect.tex}, Lemma ``Sixth-order coefficient
  arithmetic.''  The auxiliary order-eight row values are summarized in
  \path{sec_appendix.tex}, Appendix ``Finite Laurent certificate through order
  eight''; the coefficient
identification itself is the main-text finite-moment theorem, not an appendix
input.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Positive-margin quotient remainder and entropy transfer]
\label{lem:positive-margin-quotient-remainder}
\label{rem:positive-margin-retained-order}
\label{prop:density-to-entropy-descent}
\label{lem:closed-cayley-mode-constant-envelopes}
Let \(L\ge2\), \(0<\eta\le1\), and let \(\nu\) have mean
\(\bar{\gamma}\) and finite centred \((L+\eta)\)th absolute moment.  With
\[
\delta_t(y):=
\frac{(P_t*\nu)(\bar{\gamma}+ty)}{P_t(ty)}-1
\]
there is a remainder \(\rho_{L,t}^{(\eta)}\) such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:positive-margin-density-remainder}
\delta_t(y)=
\sum_{n=2}^{L}\mu_nu_n(y)t^{-n}+\rho_{L,t}^{(\eta)}(y),
\qquad
\|\rho_{L,t}^{(\eta)}\|_{\infty}
+\|\rho_{L,t}^{(\eta)}\|_{L^1(\omega)}
=O_\nu(t^{-(L+\eta)}).
\end{equation}
Moreover \(\|\delta_t\|_\infty=O_\nu(t^{-2})\).
More precisely, the remainder satisfies
\[
\|\rho_{L,t}^{(\eta)}\|_{\infty}
+\|\rho_{L,t}^{(\eta)}\|_{L^1(\omega)}
\le
C_{L,\eta}\,\EE|X_c|^{L+\eta}\,t^{-(L+\eta)},
\]
where \(C_{L,\eta}\) depends only on \(L,\eta\) and the fixed Cayley-mode
envelopes.  For \(0<\eta<1\), this is a genuine fractional-moment
improvement; for \(\eta=1\), it is the usual \((L+1)\)-moment estimate.

Consequently, if \(N\ge2\), \(L=2N-2\), and
\(\EE|X_c|^{2N-2+\eta}<\infty\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:positive-margin-retained-order-theorem}
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_t*\nu\|P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}))
=\sum_{m=2}^{N}A_{2m}(\nu)t^{-2m}
+O_\nu(t^{-(2N+\eta)}).
\end{equation}
\label{eq:explicit-entropy-constant-bound}
\end{lemma}

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
\relocatedproof{sec_entropy_core_main.tex}{the proof following Lemma
``Positive-margin quotient remainder and entropy transfer''}
\else
\begin{proof}
The quotient identity is
\[
\frac{P_t(\bar{\gamma}+ty-\gamma)}{P_t(ty)}
=R_{X_c/t}(y).
\]
Subtract the Taylor polynomial
\(\sum_{n=0}^{L}u_n(y)(X_c/t)^n\) and split the expectation into
\(|X_c|\le t\) and \(|X_c|>t\).  Both sides of the split are estimated by the
same \(L+\eta\) moment.

On \(|X_c|\le t\), the first line of
\eqref{eq:one-dimensional-two-region-taylor-remainder} gives, uniformly in
\(y\),
\[
\left|
\EE\!\left[
\left(R_{X_c/t}(y)-\sum_{n=0}^{L}u_n(y)(X_c/t)^n\right)
\mathbf1_{\{|X_c|\le t\}}
\right]\right|
\le
C_Lt^{-(L+1)}
\EE\!\left[|X_c|^{L+1}\mathbf1_{\{|X_c|\le t\}}\right].
\]
Because \(0<\eta\le1\) and \(|X_c|\le t\), the last term is bounded by
\[
C_Lt^{-(L+\eta)}
\EE\!\left[
|X_c|^{L+\eta}
\left(\frac{|X_c|}{t}\right)^{1-\eta}
\mathbf1_{\{|X_c|\le t\}}
\right]
\le
C_L\EE|X_c|^{L+\eta}\,t^{-(L+\eta)} .
\]
At the endpoint \(\eta=1\) this is precisely the assumed
\((L+1)\)-moment bound; for \(0<\eta<1\) it uses only the lower
\((L+\eta)\)-moment.

On \(|X_c|>t\), the second line of
\eqref{eq:one-dimensional-two-region-taylor-remainder} gives
\[
\left|
\EE\!\left[
\left(R_{X_c/t}(y)-\sum_{n=0}^{L}u_n(y)(X_c/t)^n\right)
\mathbf1_{\{|X_c|>t\}}
\right]\right|
\le
C_Lt^{-L}\EE\!\left[|X_c|^L\mathbf1_{\{|X_c|>t\}}\right].
\]
Since \(|X_c|>t\) implies
\(|X_c|^L\le t^{-\eta}|X_c|^{L+\eta}\), this is also
\[
O_\nu(t^{-(L+\eta)}).
\]
The same uniform bounds give the \(L^1(\omega)\) estimate because
\(\omega\) is a probability measure.  The \(n=0\) term cancels in
\(\delta_t\), and the \(n=1\) term vanishes after integration against
\(\nu\) because \(\EE X_c=0\).  This proves
\eqref{eq:positive-margin-density-remainder}.  The bound
\(\|\delta_t\|_\infty=O_\nu(t^{-2})\) follows from
Lemma~\ref{lem:exact-moment-density-truncation}, since
\(\EE|X_c|^{L+\eta}<\infty\) and \(L\ge2\).

Now put \(L=2N-2\) and
\[
d_t(y)=\sum_{n=2}^{L}\mu_nu_n(y)t^{-n}.
\]
As in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-even-orders},
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_t*\nu\|P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}))
=\int_{\RR}\Phi(\delta_t(y))\,\omega(\dd y),
\qquad
\Phi(s)=(1+s)\log(1+s)-s .
\]
The proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:finite-quotient-jet-implies-kl-coefficient-jet}, with
the stronger bound
\(\|\rho_{L,t}^{(\eta)}\|_\infty+
\|\rho_{L,t}^{(\eta)}\|_{L^1(\omega)}
=O_\nu(t^{-(L+\eta)})\), gives the same finite monomial coefficients as
Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-even-orders}; every product containing the
quotient remainder is
\(O_\nu(t^{-(L+\eta+2)})=O_\nu(t^{-(2N+\eta)})\), and the Taylor tail is
\(O_\nu(t^{-2N-2})\).  This proves
\eqref{eq:positive-margin-retained-order-theorem}.
\end{proof}
\fi

\begin{lemma}[Lower moments do not identify the next even moment]%
\label{lem:lower-moment-realizability-obstruction}
Let \(L\ge4\) be even.  There exist two centred symmetric compactly
supported probability laws \(\nu_+\) and \(\nu_-\) on \(\RR\) such that
\[
  \int x^j\,\nu_+(\dd x)=\int x^j\,\nu_-(\dd x)
  \qquad(0\le j\le L-1),
\]
but
\[
  \int x^L\,\nu_+(\dd x)\ne \int x^L\,\nu_-(\dd x).
\]
In particular, even within compactly supported centred symmetric laws, the
moment vector through order \(L-1\) does not determine the \(L\)th centred
moment.
\end{lemma}

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
\relocatedproof{sec_entropy_core_main.tex}{the proof following Lemma
``Lower moments do not identify the next even moment''}
\else
\begin{proof}
Set \(x_i=i-L/2\), \(0\le i\le L\), and define the signed measure
\[
  \sigma=\sum_{i=0}^{L}(-1)^{L-i}\binom{L}{i}\delta_{x_i}.
\]
This is the \(L\)th finite-difference functional translated by \(L/2\).
Hence, for every polynomial \(p\) of degree \(<L\),
\[
  \int p(x)\,\sigma(\dd x)
  =\sum_{i=0}^{L}(-1)^{L-i}\binom{L}{i}p(i-L/2)=0,
\]
whereas
\[
  \int x^L\,\sigma(\dd x)=L!\ne0 .
\]
Because \(L\) is even, the coefficient at \(x_i\) equals the coefficient at
\(-x_i=x_{L-i}\); thus \(\sigma\) is symmetric and has total mass zero.

Let
\[
  \lambda=\frac1{L+1}\sum_{i=0}^{L}\delta_{x_i}.
\]
Choose \(\varepsilon>0\) so small that all weights of
\(\lambda\pm\varepsilon\sigma\) are nonnegative, and put
\(\nu_\pm=\lambda\pm\varepsilon\sigma\).  Since \(\sigma(\RR)=0\), these are
probability laws.  They are symmetric, hence centred.  The moment identities
up to order \(L-1\) follow from the annihilation of lower-degree
polynomials by \(\sigma\), while their \(L\)th moments differ by
\[
  \int x^L\,\nu_+(\dd x)-\int x^L\,\nu_-(\dd x)
  =2\varepsilon L!\ne0 .
\]
\end{proof}
\fi

\begin{proposition}[Top-moment dependence of the universal coefficient]%
\label{prop:poisson-kl-moment-threshold-sharpness}
\label{prop:analytic-first-evaluability-obstruction}
For \(N\ge3\), the universal top coefficient \(A_{2N}^{\rm form}\) contains
the nonzero monomial
\[
\kappa_N\,\mathfrak m_2\mathfrak m_{2N-2},
\qquad
\kappa_N=(-1)^N(N-1)2^{-2N+2}\ne0 .
\]
More precisely, with \(L=2N-2\),
\[
  A_{2N}^{\rm form}
  =\kappa_N\,\mathfrak m_2\mathfrak m_L
  +P_N(\mathfrak m_2,\ldots,\mathfrak m_{L-1}),
\]
where \(P_N\) involves no \(\mathfrak m_L\).  Consequently the displayed
universal polynomial \(A_{2N}\) cannot be evaluated from centred moments of
order at most \(2N-3\) on the full class of laws; two compactly supported
centred symmetric laws can have the same such lower moments but different
values of \(A_{2N}\).

This is the algebraic sharpness mechanism in
Theorem~\ref{thm:sharp-moment-equivalence-hierarchy}.  It also proves the
following stronger non-identifiability statement: if the object to be
recovered is the same polynomial \(A_{2N}\) on the full finite-moment class,
then centred moments through order \(2N-3\) do not contain enough
information.  This does not assert a universal renormalized entropy residual
for arbitrary laws outside the coefficient domain.  At the regularly varying
moment boundary, however, the tail class selects a canonical integrated
slowly varying normalization; this case is closed by
Theorem~\ref{cor:main-body-conditional-missing-top-moment-tail}.  The
unconditional analytic tail threshold is the leading variance theorem,
Theorem~\ref{thm:sharp-leading-kl-variance-threshold}.
\end{proposition}

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
\relocatedproof{sec_entropy_core_main.tex}{the proof following Proposition
``Top-moment dependence of the universal coefficient''}
\else
\begin{proof}
Put \(L=2N-2\).  In the formal coefficient
\eqref{eq:universal-entropy-coefficient}, a term involving
\(\mathfrak m_L\) and contributing to total order \(2N=L+2\) must come from
the quadratic part of
\((1+s)\log(1+s)-s\), with the ordered index pairs \((2,L)\) and \((L,2)\).
All terms with three or more factors have total index at least \(2+2+2\) and,
if one factor is \(L\), total index at least \(L+4> L+2\).  Therefore
\[
  A_{2N}^{\rm form}
  =J(2,L)\,\mathfrak m_2\mathfrak m_L
  +P_N(\mathfrak m_2,\ldots,\mathfrak m_{L-1}).
\]
Writing \(L=2m\), the uniform coefficient formula
\eqref{eq:mode-laurent-coefficients} gives
\(q_{2,\pm1}=q_{2,\pm2}=-1/4\) and
\(q_{2m,\pm k}=(-1)^m2^{-2m}\binom{2m-1}{k-1}\).  Hence only the exponents
\(\pm1\) and \(\pm2\) of \(Q_{2m}\) contribute to \(J(2,2m)\), and
\[
\begin{aligned}
J(2,2m)
&=[z^0]Q_2Q_{2m}  \\
&=-\frac12\,(-1)^m2^{-2m}
\left\{\binom{2m-1}{0}+\binom{2m-1}{1}\right\}  \\
&=(-1)^{m+1}m\,2^{-2m}
=(-1)^N(N-1)2^{-2N+2}.
\end{aligned}
\]
This proves the displayed top-moment coefficient.

It remains to justify the stated lower-moment non-identifiability.  Apply
Lemma~\ref{lem:lower-moment-realizability-obstruction} with this
\(L=2N-2\).  The resulting compactly supported symmetric laws \(\nu_+\) and
\(\nu_-\) have the same centred moments through order \(L-1\), in particular
the same positive second moment, but they have different \(L\)th centred
moments.  Since
\[
  A_{2N}(\nu_+)-A_{2N}(\nu_-)
  =\kappa_N\,\mu_2\{\mu_L(\nu_+)-\mu_L(\nu_-)\}
  \ne0,
\]
no rule depending only on centred moments of orders at most \(L-1=2N-3\)
can evaluate \(A_{2N}\) on the full class of laws.
\end{proof}
\fi

\begin{theorem}[Regularly varying missing-moment boundary layer]%
\label{cor:main-body-conditional-missing-top-moment-tail}
Let \(N\ge3\), put \(p=2N-2\), and let \(X\) have a centred probability
law \(\nu\) on \(\RR\).  Assume that, for some measurable eventually
positive slowly varying function \(L\) and constants \(c_+,c_-\ge0\) with
\(C:=c_++c_->0\),
\[
 \frac{\mathbb P(X>x)}{x^{-p}L(x)}\longrightarrow c_+,\qquad
 \frac{\mathbb P(X<-x)}{x^{-p}L(x)}\longrightarrow c_-,
 \qquad x\to\infty.
\]
Fix \(x_0>0\) beyond which \(L\) is positive, and assume that
\[
 \ell_L(t):=\int_{x_0}^{t}\frac{L(s)}s\,\dd s\longrightarrow\infty.
\]
Then \(\EE|X|^q<\infty\) for every \(q<p\), whereas
\(\EE|X|^p=\infty\), and the truncated boundary moment satisfies
\[
 M_p(t):=\EE\!\left[|X|^p\mathbf1_{\{|X|\le t\}}\right]
 \sim pC\ell_L(t).
\]
With \(H(t)=D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_t*\nu\|P_t)\) and
\(\kappa_N=(-1)^N(N-1)2^{-2N+2}\), one has the exact boundary expansion
\[
H(t)=
\sum_{j=2}^{N-1}A_{2j}(\nu)t^{-2j}
+\kappa_N\mu_2\,t^{-2N}M_p(t)
+o(t^{-2N}M_p(t)).
\]
Equivalently,
\[
 \lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{t^{2N}}{M_p(t)}
 \left\{H(t)-\sum_{j=2}^{N-1}A_{2j}(\nu)t^{-2j}\right\}
 =\kappa_N\mu_2,
\]
and
\[
 \lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{t^{2N}}{\ell_L(t)}
 \left\{H(t)-\sum_{j=2}^{N-1}A_{2j}(\nu)t^{-2j}\right\}
 =p(c_++c_-)\kappa_N\mu_2.
\]
Thus the leading boundary layer depends on the sum of the two tail constants

 succeeded in 181ms:
but not on their balance.  In particular, \(L\equiv1\) gives a \(\log t\)
correction, while \(L(t)=(\log t)^{-1}\) gives a \(\log\log t\)
correction.  No density, symmetry, or monotonicity of \(L\) is assumed.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Sharp leading threshold}

\begin{lemma}[Second-mode transform of a Cauchy translate]%
\label{lem:second-mode-cauchy-translate-transform}
For \(\varepsilon\in\RR\),
\[
\int_{\RR}u_2(y)\{R_\varepsilon(y)-1\}\,\omega(\dd y)
=\frac{4\varepsilon^2}{(4+\varepsilon^2)^2}.
\]
\end{lemma}

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
\relocatedproof{sec_entropy_core_main.tex}{the proof following Lemma
``Second-mode transform of a Cauchy translate''}
\else
\begin{proof}
Since \(\int_{\RR}u_2\,\dd\omega=0\), it is enough to compute
\(\int u_2R_\varepsilon\,\dd\omega\).  We use the same Cayley/Fourier
normalization as in Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-fourier-mode-integral-algorithm}.
Put \(y=\tan\theta\) and \(z=e^{2\ii\theta}\).  Then
\(\omega(\dd y)=\dd\theta/\pi\), Haar integration on the \(z\)-circle is
constant-term extraction, and
\[
  Q_2(z)=u_2(\tan\theta)
  =-\frac14(z+z^{-1}+z^2+z^{-2}).
\]
For \(\varepsilon=0\), \(R_0\equiv1\) and the integral is zero.  Assume
\(\varepsilon\ne0\).  From
\[
R_\varepsilon(\tan\theta)
=\{1-\varepsilon\sin2\theta+\varepsilon^2\cos^2\theta\}^{-1}
\]
one obtains, after multiplying numerator and denominator by \(z\),
\[
R_\varepsilon(\tan\theta)
=
\frac{z}
{\frac{\varepsilon(\varepsilon+2\ii)}4z^2
+(1+\varepsilon^2/2)z
+\frac{\varepsilon(\varepsilon-2\ii)}4}
=
\frac{\alpha}{z-\alpha}-\frac{\beta}{z-\beta},
\]
where
\[
\alpha=-\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon+2\ii},
\qquad
\beta=\frac{2\ii-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}.
\]
Here \(|\alpha|<1<|\beta|\), so on the unit circle the Laurent expansion is
\[
R_\varepsilon(\tan\theta)
=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\beta^{-n}z^n
+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\alpha^nz^{-n}.
\]
Hence
\[
\begin{aligned}
\int_{\RR}u_2(y)R_\varepsilon(y)\,\omega(\dd y)
&=[z^0]\,Q_2(z)R_\varepsilon(\tan\theta)\\
&=-\frac14\left(\alpha+\beta^{-1}+\alpha^2+\beta^{-2}\right)\\
&=-\frac14\left\{
-\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon+2\ii}
-\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon-2\ii}
+\frac{\varepsilon^2}{(\varepsilon+2\ii)^2}
+\frac{\varepsilon^2}{(\varepsilon-2\ii)^2}
\right\}  \\
&=-\frac14\left\{
-\frac{2\varepsilon^2}{4+\varepsilon^2}
+\frac{2\varepsilon^2(\varepsilon^2-4)}
{(4+\varepsilon^2)^2}
\right\}  \\
&=-\frac14\left\{
-\frac{16\varepsilon^2}{(4+\varepsilon^2)^2}
\right\}  \\
&=\frac{4\varepsilon^2}{(4+\varepsilon^2)^2}.
\end{aligned}
\]
The last line is an elementary rational simplification; equivalently, the
bracket equals \(-16\varepsilon^2/(4+\varepsilon^2)^2\).  This proves the
claimed transform.
\end{proof}
\fi

\begin{theorem}[Sharp analytic variance threshold for the leading KL coefficient]%
\label{thm:sharp-leading-kl-variance-threshold}
Let \(\nu\) have finite mean \(\bar{\gamma}\), and put
\[
H(t)=D_{\rm KL}(P_t*\nu\|P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma})).
\]
With the extended nonnegative convention
\eqref{eq:extended-kl-definition} for \(D_{\rm KL}\),
\[
H(t)\in[0,\infty]\qquad(t>0).
\]
Then
\[
\EE X_c^2<\infty
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\limsup_{t\to\infty}t^4H(t)<\infty .
\]
When the variance is finite,
\[
H(t)<\infty\qquad(t>0)
\]
and
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}t^4H(t)=\frac{(\EE X_c^2)^2}{8},
\]
and when the variance is infinite,
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}t^4H(t)=+\infty .
\]
The last limit is meant in the extended sense; no finite-time finiteness of
\(H(t)\) is asserted in the infinite-variance case, and the conclusion
includes the case \(H(t)=+\infty\) along an unbounded set of times.
No compact-support, exponential-tail, or regular-variation hypothesis is
assumed; finite variance is exactly one of the two equivalent conclusions.
This is the unconditional analytic iff asserted in this paper.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Finite variance is Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-even-orders} with \(N=2\).
In that case Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-time-poisson-kl-finite} also gives
\(H(t)<\infty\) for every \(t>0\).  Hence the finite-variance limit is an
ordinary real limit.

For the converse, write
\[
h_t=P_t*\nu,\qquad g_t=P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}),\qquad
\delta_t(y)=\frac{h_t(\bar{\gamma}+ty)}{g_t(\bar{\gamma}+ty)}-1 .
\]
Let \(\varphi_t(x)=u_2((x-\bar{\gamma})/t)\).  Since
\(\|\varphi_t\|_\infty=\|u_2\|_\infty\le1\),
Lemma~\ref{lem:bounded-test-pinsker} gives, in the
extended-valued sense,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bounded-test-pinsker-second-mode}
2H(t)\ge
\left|
\int_{\RR}\varphi_t(x)\{h_t(x)-g_t(x)\}\dd x
\right|^2
=
\left|
\int_{\RR}u_2(y)\delta_t(y)\,\omega(\dd y)
\right|^2 .
\end{equation}
This use of Pinsker involves only the bounded test \(\varphi_t\); it does not
assume that \(H(t)\) is finite.

It remains to identify the projection without using an \(X_c^2\)-integrability
hypothesis.  For fixed \(t>0\),
\[
\delta_t(y)=\int_{\RR}\{R_{X_c/t}(y)-1\}\,\dd\nu(\gamma),
\]
where the integrand is bounded as a function of \(\gamma\) for each fixed
\(y\).  Moreover
\[
R_\varepsilon(y)\omega(\dd y)
=\frac{\dd y}{\pi(1+(y-\varepsilon)^2)},
\qquad
\int_{\RR}|R_\varepsilon-1|\,\dd\omega\le2 .
\]
Since \(\|u_2\|_\infty\le1\), Tonelli gives the uniform bound
\[
\int_{\RR}\!\!\int_{\RR}
|u_2(y)|\,|R_{X_c/t}(y)-1|\,\omega(\dd y)\,\nu(\dd\gamma)
\le2 .
\]
Thus Fubini is justified against the possibly infinite-variance law \(\nu\),
and Lemma~\ref{lem:second-mode-cauchy-translate-transform} gives
\[
B(t):=
\int_{\RR}u_2(y)\delta_t(y)\,\omega(\dd y)
=
\EE\!\left[\frac{4(X_c/t)^2}{(4+(X_c/t)^2)^2}\right]\ge0 .
\]
Combining this identity with \eqref{eq:bounded-test-pinsker-second-mode}
yields
\[
t^4H(t)\ge\frac12\{t^2B(t)\}^2 .
\]
For fixed \(M<\infty\),
\[
t^2B(t)\ge
\frac{4}{(4+(M/t)^2)^2}
\EE[X_c^2\mathbf1_{\{|X_c|\le M\}}],
\]
and the right side tends to
\(\frac14\EE[X_c^2\mathbf1_{\{|X_c|\le M\}}]\).  Letting
\(M\to\infty\) proves divergence when the variance is infinite.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
This theorem supplies the threshold clause in Theorem~\ref{thm:main-A}.
Its proof uses the coefficient theorem only in the finite-variance direction;
the infinite-variance direction is a bounded-test lower bound and does not
assert a finite Laurent expansion.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Multidimensional quotient and covariance package}

For \(d\ge1\), let
\[
P_t^{(d)}(x)=c_d\,\frac{t}{(t^2+|x|^2)^{(d+1)/2}},
\qquad

 succeeded in 181ms:
\(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}\).
Contracting \(i=j\) and \(k=l\) gives
\[
1=\EE_\Theta|\Theta|^4=C(d^2+2d),
\]
so \(C=1/(d(d+2))\), proving
\eqref{eq:spherical-fourth-contraction}.  Contracting this identity with
\(A_{ij}B_{kl}\) gives \eqref{eq:spherical-quadratic-contraction}; taking
\(\operatorname{tr}A=0\) and \(B=A\) gives
\eqref{eq:spherical-traceless-square}.
\end{proof}
\fi

\subsubsection*{Algebraic covariance layer}\leavevmode\par\smallskip

The following theorem evaluates the finite quadratic form determined by the
degree-two density mode.  The proof is written directly at the quotient level:
first the homogeneous degree-two part of
\((1+(-2y\cdot z+|z|^2)/(1+|y|^2))^{-(d+1)/2}\) is extracted, and only then
is the covariance average taken.

\begin{theorem}[Covariance algebra under finite covariance]%
\label{thm:multidim-covariance-algebra}
\label{prop:multidim-formal-covariance-layer}
Let \(d\ge1\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability measure on \(\RR^d\) with
finite centred second moment.  Put
\[
\bar{\gamma}=\int_{\RR^d}\gamma\,\dd\nu(\gamma),
\qquad
X_c=\gamma-\bar{\gamma},
\]
and
\[
\Sigma=\EE[X_cX_c^{\mathsf T}],\qquad
\Sigma_0=\Sigma-\frac{\operatorname{tr}\Sigma}{d}I_d.
\]
The degree-two centred density mode associated with \(\Sigma\) is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:multidim-leading-theorem-density-mode}
b_\Sigma(y)=
\frac{d+1}{2}\left(
-\frac{\operatorname{tr}\Sigma}{1+|y|^2}
+\frac{(d+3)y^{\mathsf T}\Sigma y}{(1+|y|^2)^2}
\right).
\end{equation}
Define its algebraic quadratic entropy-integrand layer by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:multidim-leading-positive-defect}
\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
:=\frac12\int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)^2\,\Omega_d(\dd y)
=c_d^{\rm iso}(\operatorname{tr}\Sigma)^2
+c_d^{\rm tr}\|\Sigma_0\|_{\rm HS}^2,
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:multidim-leading-positive-constants}
c_d^{\rm iso}=
\frac{3(d+1)(7d+9)}
{4d(d+3)(d+5)(d+7)},
\qquad
c_d^{\rm tr}=
\frac{3(d+1)(d+3)}
{4(d+5)(d+7)} .
\end{equation}
Two immediate checks are
\[
d=1:\qquad \Sigma_0=0,\qquad c_1^{\rm iso}=\frac18,\qquad
\mathcal Q_1(\Sigma)=\frac{(\operatorname{tr}\Sigma)^2}{8},
\]
and, for an isotropic covariance \(\Sigma=\tau I_d\),
\[
\Sigma_0=0,\qquad
\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)=c_d^{\rm iso}d^2\tau^2 .
\]
This is the algebraic covariance calculation.  No KL asymptotic is asserted
in this theorem.  Lemma~\ref{lem:radial-angular-contractions} gives the radial
and angular contractions entering the constants.
\end{theorem}

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
\relocatedproof{sec_entropy_core_main.tex}{the proof following Theorem
``Covariance algebra under finite covariance''}
\else
\begin{proof}
First compute the formal degree-two density layer.  Let
\[
  a=\frac{d+1}{2},\qquad A=1+|y|^2 .
\]
For a deterministic \(z\in\RR^d\), the radial Poisson quotient is
\[
F_y(z):=
\left(\frac{1+|y|^2}{1+|y-z|^2}\right)^a
=
\left(1+\frac{-2y\cdot z+|z|^2}{A}\right)^{-a}.
\]
Write
\[
  w=\frac{-2y\cdot z+|z|^2}{A}=w_1+w_2,\qquad
  w_1=-\frac{2y\cdot z}{A},\qquad
  w_2=\frac{|z|^2}{A}.
\]
The binomial expansion gives
\[
  (1+w)^{-a}=1-aw+\frac{a(a+1)}2w^2+O(|w|^3),
\]
and the homogeneous degree-two contribution comes only from \(-aw_2\) and
\(\frac{a(a+1)}2w_1^2\).  Hence that degree-two part is
\[
a\left(-\frac{|z|^2}{A}
+\frac{(d+3)(y\cdot z)^2}{A^2}\right).
\]
Indeed,
\[
  -aw_2+\frac{a(a+1)}2w_1^2
  =
  -a\frac{|z|^2}{A}
  +2a(a+1)\frac{(y\cdot z)^2}{A^2},
\]
and \(2(a+1)=d+3\).  No other term contributes at degree two:
\(-aw_1\) is degree one, \(w_1w_2\) is degree three, and \(w_2^2\) is degree
four.  The degree-zero term is the matched reference density and the
degree-one term vanishes after centering.  Averaging the displayed
degree-two term with \(z=X_c/t\) gives
\[
  D_2^{(d)}(y;\nu)=b_\Sigma(y)=
  \frac{d+1}{2}\left(
  -\frac{\operatorname{tr}\Sigma}{1+|y|^2}
  +\frac{(d+3)y^{\mathsf T}\Sigma y}{(1+|y|^2)^2}
  \right),
\]
since
\[
\EE |X_c|^2=\operatorname{tr}\Sigma,\qquad
\EE (y\cdot X_c)^2=y^{\mathsf T}\Sigma y .
\]

It remains to evaluate \(\frac12\int b_\Sigma^2\,\dd\Omega_d\).  Decompose
\(\Sigma=\tau I_d+\Sigma_0\), \(\tau=d^{-1}\operatorname{tr}\Sigma\).  Then
\[
b_\Sigma(y)=
a\tau\frac{3|y|^2-d}{(1+|y|^2)^2}
+a(d+3)\frac{y^{\mathsf T}\Sigma_0y}{(1+|y|^2)^2}.
\]
Let \(Y=\sqrt R\,\Theta\) be distributed according to \(\Omega_d\).  In these
variables,
\[
b_\Sigma(Y)=
a\tau\frac{3R-d}{(1+R)^2}
+a(d+3)\frac{R\,\Theta^{\mathsf T}\Sigma_0\Theta}{(1+R)^2}.
\]
The mixed term in \(\EE b_\Sigma(Y)^2\) is zero because \(R\) and \(\Theta\)
are independent and
\[
\EE_\Theta[\Theta^{\mathsf T}\Sigma_0\Theta]
=\frac{\operatorname{tr}\Sigma_0}{d}=0 .
\]

The isotropic part uses only the low radial moments from
Lemma~\ref{lem:radial-angular-contractions}.  With
\[
  D_d=(d+1)(d+3)(d+5)(d+7),
\]
those moments are
\[
  \EE\frac1{(1+R)^4}=\frac{105}{D_d},\qquad
  \EE\frac{R}{(1+R)^4}=\frac{15d}{D_d},\qquad
  \EE\frac{R^2}{(1+R)^4}=\frac{3d(d+2)}{D_d}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\begin{aligned}
\EE\frac{(3R-d)^2}{(1+R)^4}
&=
9\EE\frac{R^2}{(1+R)^4}
-6d\EE\frac{R}{(1+R)^4}
+d^2\EE\frac1{(1+R)^4} \\
&=
\frac{27d(d+2)-90d^2+105d^2}{D_d} \\
&=
\frac{6d(7d+9)}
{(d+1)(d+3)(d+5)(d+7)} .
\end{aligned}
\]
The isotropic contribution to \(\frac12\int b_\Sigma^2\,\dd\Omega_d\) is
\[
\begin{aligned}
\frac12 a^2\frac{(\operatorname{tr}\Sigma)^2}{d^2}
\EE\frac{(3R-d)^2}{(1+R)^4}
&=
\frac{3(d+1)(7d+9)}
{4d(d+3)(d+5)(d+7)}
(\operatorname{tr}\Sigma)^2 .
\end{aligned}
\]
For the traceless part, the radial and angular factors separate.  The
spherical fourth-moment contraction gives
\[
\EE_\Theta[(\Theta^{\mathsf T}\Sigma_0\Theta)^2]
=\frac{2}{d(d+2)}\|\Sigma_0\|_{\rm HS}^2,
\]
because \(\operatorname{tr}\Sigma_0=0\).  Hence
\[
\begin{aligned}
\int_{\RR^d}
\frac{(y^{\mathsf T}\Sigma_0y)^2}{(1+|y|^2)^4}\,\Omega_d(\dd y)
&=
\EE\frac{R^2}{(1+R)^4}\,
\EE_\Theta[(\Theta^{\mathsf T}\Sigma_0\Theta)^2] \\
&=
\frac{3d(d+2)}{D_d}\,
\frac{2}{d(d+2)}
\|\Sigma_0\|_{\rm HS}^2 \\
&=
\frac{6}{(d+1)(d+3)(d+5)(d+7)}
\|\Sigma_0\|_{\rm HS}^2 .
\end{aligned}
\]
Here the first factor is the \(k=2\) value in
\eqref{eq:radial-beta-prime-contractions-low}, and the second factor is the
traceless angular contraction
\eqref{eq:spherical-traceless-square}.
Multiplication by \(\frac12a^2(d+3)^2\) gives
\[
\frac{3(d+1)(d+3)}{4(d+5)(d+7)}
\|\Sigma_0\|_{\rm HS}^2 .
\]
This proves \eqref{eq:multidim-leading-positive-constants}.  The constants are
positive, so the layer vanishes exactly when \(\Sigma=0\).
\end{proof}
\fi

\subsubsection*{Second-order quotient bridge}\leavevmode\par\smallskip

The next statements record a second-order quotient criterion for the leading
coefficient.  The finite \(d+1\)-moment theorem supplies the uniform version.

\noindent The following lemma shows that \(L^2\) convergence alone controls
the nonlinear part of a non-uniform second-order entropy transfer.

\begin{lemma}[\(L^2\) second-order entropy criterion]%
\label{lem:l2-second-order-entropy-criterion}
Let \((X,\mathcal A,\lambda)\) be a probability space and let
\(\delta_t:X\to[-1,\infty)\) be measurable with
\(\int_X\delta_t\,\dd\lambda=0\).  Let \(b\in L^2(\lambda)\), and put
\[
  \Phi(s)=(1+s)\log(1+s)-s .
\]
Assume only
\[
  t^2\delta_t\longrightarrow b
  \qquad\text{in }L^2(\lambda).
\]
Then
\[
  \int_X\Phi(\delta_t)\,\dd\lambda
  =\frac{t^{-4}}2\int_X b^2\,\dd\lambda+o(t^{-4}).
\]
\end{lemma}

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
\relocatedproof{sec_entropy_core_main.tex}{the proof following Lemma
``\(L^2\) second-order entropy criterion''}
\else
\begin{proof}
Write \(z_t=t^2\delta_t\), and define
\[
r(s)=
\begin{cases}
\dfrac{\Phi(s)-s^2/2}{s^2},&s\ne0,\\[4pt]
0,&s=0.
\end{cases}
\]
The function \(r\) is bounded on \([-1,\infty)\) and continuous at zero.
Indeed, the Taylor expansion at zero gives \(r(s)\to0\), while the limits at
\(-1\) and at infinity are finite.  The \(L^2\) convergence gives
\[
  \int_X z_t^2\,\dd\lambda\to\int_X b^2\,\dd\lambda .
\]
It also implies that \(z_t^2\to b^2\) in \(L^1(\lambda)\), so
\(\{z_t^2\}\) is uniformly integrable, whereas
\(z_t/t^2=\delta_t\to0\) in probability.  Boundedness of \(r\) and Vitali's
theorem therefore yield
\[
t^4\int_X\left|\Phi(\delta_t)-\frac12\delta_t^2\right|\dd\lambda
=\int_X\left|r\!\left(\frac{z_t}{t^2}\right)\right|z_t^2\dd\lambda
\longrightarrow0.
\]
Thus
\[
  \frac12\int_X\delta_t^2\,\dd\lambda
  =\frac{t^{-4}}2\int_Xz_t^2\,\dd\lambda
  =\frac{t^{-4}}2\int_Xb^2\,\dd\lambda+o(t^{-4}).
\]
Combining the last two displays proves the claim.
\end{proof}
\fi

\medskip

\begin{theorem}[Second-order quotient criterion for the KL coefficient]%
\label{thm:second-order-quotient-kl-coefficient}
\label{prop:second-order-quotient-criterion}
Let \(d\ge1\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability measure on \(\RR^d\) with
mean \(\bar{\gamma}\) and finite centred covariance \(\Sigma\).  Put
\[
  h_t=P_t^{(d)}*\nu,\qquad
  g_t=P_t^{(d)}(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}),\qquad
  \delta_t^{(d)}(y)=\frac{h_t(\bar{\gamma}+ty)}
  {g_t(\bar{\gamma}+ty)}-1 .
\]
Assume the separate second-order quotient criterion
\begin{equation}\label{eq:second-order-quotient-criterion}
  \delta_t^{(d)}(y)=t^{-2}b_\Sigma(y)+r_t(y),\qquad
  \|r_t\|_\infty+\|r_t\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}=o(t^{-2}),\qquad
  \|\delta_t^{(d)}\|_\infty=O(t^{-2})
\end{equation}
with \(b_\Sigma\) as in
\eqref{eq:multidim-leading-theorem-density-mode}, and assume that the matched
KL integral is represented by
\[
D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|g_t)
=\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(\delta_t^{(d)}(y))\,\Omega_d(\dd y)
\]
for all sufficiently large \(t\).  Then

\section{Regularly varying missing-moment boundary layer}%
\label{app:conditional-missing-top-moment-tails}

This appendix proves
Theorem~\ref{cor:main-body-conditional-missing-top-moment-tail}.  The proof
has two distinct inputs.  Karamata theory identifies the divergent truncated
boundary moment and controls all omitted tails.  The Cayley quotient estimate
then transfers that single truncated mode through the entropy Taylor map.
Neither an absolute-continuity hypothesis nor a symmetry hypothesis is
needed.

Throughout the appendix, let \(N\ge3\), \(p=2N-2\), and let \(X\) be
centred.  Write
\[
 \overline F(t):=\mathbb P(|X|>t),\qquad
 C:=c_++c_->0,
\]
and suppose that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rv-boundary-absolute-tail}
 \overline F(t)\sim Ct^{-p}L(t).
\end{equation}
The two one-sided hypotheses in the theorem imply
\eqref{eq:rv-boundary-absolute-tail}; their balance will not otherwise enter
the proof.

\begin{lemma}[Regularly varying boundary-moment package]%
\label{lem:rv-boundary-moment-package}
Let \(p>2\), let \(L\) be measurable, eventually positive, and slowly
varying, and assume \eqref{eq:rv-boundary-absolute-tail}.  Put
\[
 \ell_L(t):=\int_{x_0}^t\frac{L(s)}s\,\dd s
\]
and suppose \(\ell_L(t)\to\infty\).  Then \(\ell_L\) is slowly
varying, \(L(t)=o(\ell_L(t))\), and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rv-truncated-p-moment}
 M_p(t):=\EE[|X|^p\mathbf1_{\{|X|\le t\}}]
 \sim pC\ell_L(t).
\end{equation}
In particular, \(\EE|X|^q<\infty\) for \(q<p\),
\(\EE|X|^p=\infty\), \(M_p(t)\to\infty\), and
\(M_p(t)=o(t^{p-2})\).  Moreover, for each \(0\le q<p\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rv-lower-omitted-tail}
 t^{-q}\EE[|X|^q\mathbf1_{\{|X|>t\}}]
 =O(t^{-p}L(t)),
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rv-local-p-plus-one}
 t^{-(p+1)}
 \EE[|X|^{p+1}\mathbf1_{\{|X|\le t\}}]
 =O(t^{-p}L(t)).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The integrated slowly varying assertions
\[
 \ell_L\in\mathrm{SV},\qquad L(t)=o(\ell_L(t))
\]
are the divergent-integral case of the standard integrated-slow-variation
theorem; see
\cite[Proposition~1.5.9a]{BinghamGoldieTeugels1987RegularVariation}.
Karamata's direct and tail theorems are used below in the normalization of
\cite[Theorem~1.5.11]{BinghamGoldieTeugels1987RegularVariation}.

For a nonnegative random variable \(Y\), integration by parts gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rv-truncated-layer-cake}
 \EE[Y^r\mathbf1_{\{Y\le t\}}]
 =r\int_0^t s^{r-1}\mathbb P(Y>s)\,\dd s
 -t^r\mathbb P(Y>t).
\end{equation}
Apply this with \(Y=|X|\) and \(r=p\).  Since
\(s^{p-1}\overline F(s)\sim Cs^{-1}L(s)\), the index \(-1\) case of
Karamata's direct theorem
\cite[Theorem~1.5.11]{BinghamGoldieTeugels1987RegularVariation}, together
with the divergence of \(\ell_L\), gives
\[
 \int_{x_0}^t s^{p-1}\overline F(s)\,\dd s
 \sim C\ell_L(t).
\]
Here the choice of \(x_0\) changes \(\ell_L\) only by an additive constant,
which is absorbed by the divergent slowly varying scale
\(\ell_L\in\mathrm{SV}\).
The terminal term in \eqref{eq:rv-truncated-layer-cake} is asymptotic to
\(CL(t)=o(\ell_L(t))\), proving
\eqref{eq:rv-truncated-p-moment}.  The same layer-cake identity without
truncation shows that moments of order \(q<p\) are finite and that the
\(p\)th moment diverges.  Since every slowly varying function is
\(o(t^\varepsilon)\) for each \(\varepsilon>0\),
\(M_p(t)=o(t^{p-2})\).

For \(0\le q<p\), another integration by parts yields
\[
 \EE[|X|^q\mathbf1_{\{|X|>t\}}]
 =t^q\overline F(t)
 +q\int_t^\infty s^{q-1}\overline F(s)\,\dd s
 =O(t^{q-p}L(t)),
\]
where Karamata's tail theorem applies because \(q-p-1<-1\).  This is
\eqref{eq:rv-lower-omitted-tail}.  Finally, taking \(r=p+1\) in
\eqref{eq:rv-truncated-layer-cake} and using Karamata's direct theorem at
index zero gives
\[
 \EE[|X|^{p+1}\mathbf1_{\{|X|\le t\}}]=O(tL(t)),
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:rv-local-p-plus-one}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Unconditional regularly varying quotient jet]%
\label{lem:rv-boundary-quotient-jet}
Under the hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{cor:main-body-conditional-missing-top-moment-tail}, define
\[
 \Delta_t(y):=\frac{(P_t*\nu)(ty)}{P_t(ty)}-1,\qquad
 A_t(y):=\sum_{n=2}^{p-1}\mu_nu_n(y)t^{-n}.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rv-boundary-quotient-jet}
 \Delta_t(y)=A_t(y)+u_p(y)t^{-p}M_p(t)+e_t(y),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rv-boundary-quotient-error}
 \|e_t\|_\infty+\|e_t\|_{L^1(\omega)}
 =O(t^{-p}L(t))
 =o(t^{-p}M_p(t)).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The identity
\[
 \Delta_t(y)=\EE\{R_{X/t}(y)-1\}
\]
holds for every probability law.  On \(|X|\le t\), expand
\(R_{X/t}\) through order \(p\).  The uniform Cayley remainder in
Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform-one-dimensional-cayley-taylor-remainder} and
\eqref{eq:rv-local-p-plus-one} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rv-local-quotient-error}
 \left\|
 \EE\!\left[
 \left\{R_{X/t}(y)-\sum_{n=0}^pu_n(y)(X/t)^n\right\}
 \mathbf1_{\{|X|\le t\}}\right]
 \right\|_\infty
 =O(t^{-p}L(t)).
\end{equation}
The same estimate holds in \(L^1(\omega)\), because \(\omega\) is a
probability measure.

The centred first moment vanishes.  For each \(1\le n<p\), completing the
truncated moment to \(\mu_n\) costs, by
\eqref{eq:rv-lower-omitted-tail} and the boundedness of \(u_n\),
\[
 \left\|u_nt^{-n}
 \EE[X^n\mathbf1_{\{|X|>t\}}]\right\|_\infty
 =O(t^{-p}L(t)).
\]
There are only finitely many such terms.  The top local term is exactly
\(u_pt^{-p}M_p(t)\), since \(p\) is even.

It remains to bound the unexpanded far quotient.  The elementary estimate
\[
 |R_\varepsilon(y)-1|\le C_0(1+\varepsilon^2),
 \qquad y,\varepsilon\in\RR,
\]
and \eqref{eq:rv-lower-omitted-tail} at \(q=0,2\) give
\[
 \left\|\EE[
 \{R_{X/t}(y)-1\}\mathbf1_{\{|X|>t\}}]\right\|_\infty
 \le C_0\left\{\mathbb P(|X|>t)
 +t^{-2}\EE[|X|^2\mathbf1_{\{|X|>t\}}]\right\}
 =O(t^{-p}L(t)).
\]
Again the uniform bound implies the same \(L^1(\omega)\) bound.
Collecting the local remainder, lower-moment completions, and far quotient
proves the first equality in \eqref{eq:rv-boundary-quotient-error}.  Its
little-oh form follows from
\(L(t)=o(\ell_L(t))\) and
\(M_p(t)\sim pC\ell_L(t)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of
Theorem~\ref{cor:main-body-conditional-missing-top-moment-tail}]
Lemma~\ref{lem:rv-boundary-moment-package} proves all moment assertions and
the asymptotic \(M_p(t)\sim pC\ell_L(t)\).  By
Lemma~\ref{lem:rv-boundary-quotient-jet}, the quotient has the form required
by the moving-scale Taylor transfer
Lemma~\ref{lem:moving-scale-taylor-transfer}, with
\[
 a_2=\mu_2u_2,\qquad b=u_p.
\]
The finite second moment supplied by
Lemma~\ref{lem:rv-boundary-moment-package} also gives the finite KL
representation \(H(t)=\int\Phi(\Delta_t)\,\dd\omega\) by
Lemma~\ref{lem:kl-bounded-test-and-poisson-quotient}.  Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rv-entropy-transfer}
 H(t)=\int_\RR\Phi(A_t)\,\dd\omega
 +\mu_2J(2,p)t^{-(p+2)}M_p(t)
 +o(t^{-(p+2)}M_p(t)).
\end{equation}
The finite Laurent Taylor algebra and its odd-parity cancellation give
\[
 \int_\RR\Phi(A_t)\,\dd\omega
 =\sum_{j=2}^{N-1}A_{2j}(\nu)t^{-2j}+O(t^{-2N}).
\]
The final error is
\(o(t^{-2N}M_p(t))\), because \(M_p(t)\to\infty\).  Finally,
Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-kl-moment-threshold-sharpness} computes
\[
 J(2,p)=(-1)^N(N-1)2^{-2N+2}=\kappa_N.
\]
Substitution into \eqref{eq:rv-entropy-transfer} proves the expansion and
the first normalized limit.  Division by
\(\ell_L(t)\), followed by
\(M_p(t)/\ell_L(t)\to p(c_++c_-)\), proves the second.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Sharp scope of the boundary hypothesis.}
The regular-variation index alone does not imply that the \(p\)th moment
diverges.  For example,
\[
 \mathbb P(|X|>t)\sim Ct^{-p}(\log t)^{-a}
\]
has an infinite \(p\)th moment for \(a\le1\), with boundary corrections
\[
 \ell_L(t)\sim
 \begin{cases}
 (\log t)^{1-a}/(1-a),&a<1,\\
 \log\log t,&a=1,
 \end{cases}
\]
but has a finite \(p\)th moment for \(a>1\).  In the latter case the
ordinary coefficient theorem applies and there is no divergent
slowly varying replacement.  For tails that are not regularly varying, or
for second-order remainders beyond the leading \(\ell_L\) scale, the
present theorem makes no assertion; such refinements require corresponding
first- or second-order regular-variation hypotheses.

% sec_covariance_proxy_defect.tex -- exact covariance-proxy KL defect
\section{The Covariance-Matched Poisson Defect}
\label{sec:covariance-proxy-defect}

Finite covariance determines the quadratic mode but, in dimensions at least
four, does not force the matched entropy to attain the corresponding
coefficient.  The following result identifies the complete nonnegative
remainder without imposing a tail class or an absolute moment above order
two.

\begin{theorem}[Covariance-proxy defect decomposition]
\label{thm:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}
\label{thm:finite-covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}
Let \(d\ge1\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR^d\) with mean
\(\bar\gamma\) and finite covariance matrix \(\Sigma\).  Put
\[
 h_t=P_t^{(d)}*\nu,
 \qquad g_t=P_t^{(d)}(\mathord\cdot-\bar\gamma),
 \qquad H_d(t)=D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|g_t),
\]
and let \(b_\Sigma\) be the bounded covariance mode in
\eqref{eq:multidim-leading-theorem-density-mode}.  For all sufficiently
large \(t\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-poisson-proxy}
 k_{\Sigma,t}(x)
 :=g_t(x)\left[1+t^{-2}b_\Sigma
 \left(\frac{x-\bar\gamma}{t}\right)\right]
\end{equation}
is a probability density.  Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-functional}
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)
 :=t^4D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|k_{\Sigma,t}).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}
 t^4H_d(t)
 =\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)+\mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)+o(1)
 \qquad(t\to\infty).
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-equivalence}
 t^4H_d(t)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
No density of \(\nu\), regular-variation hypothesis, angular balance, or
higher absolute moment is required.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
After translation, assume \(\bar\gamma=0\), and write
\[
 \Omega_d(\dd y)=P_1^{(d)}(y)\dd y,
 \qquad
 F_y(z)=\left(\frac{1+|y|^2}{1+|y-z|^2}\right)^{(d+1)/2}.
\]
In normalized coordinates,
\[
 q_t(y):=\frac{h_t(ty)}{g_t(ty)}
 =\EE F_y(X/t),
 \qquad \delta_t=q_t-1,
 \qquad u_t=t^{-2}b_\Sigma.
\]
The covariance calculation in
Theorem~\ref{thm:multidim-covariance-algebra} gives
\(\int b_\Sigma\dd\Omega_d=0\).  Since \(b_\Sigma\) is bounded,
\(1+u_t\ge1/2\) for all sufficiently large \(t\), proving that
\eqref{eq:covariance-poisson-proxy} is positive and has integral one.

We first establish the only asymptotic input needed below:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-weak-pairing}
 t^2\int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)\delta_t(y)\,\Omega_d(\dd y)
 \longrightarrow
 \int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)^2\,\Omega_d(\dd y).
\end{equation}
Set
\[
 J(z)=\int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)F_y(z)\,\Omega_d(\dd y)
 =\int_{\RR^d}b_\Sigma(y)P_1^{(d)}(y-z)\dd y.
\]
The function \(b_\Sigma\) is even, smooth, and has bounded second
derivatives.  Translation in the last integral shows that \(J\) is even,
\(J(0)=0\), and its Hessian is bounded on the unit ball.  Hence
\(|J(z)|\le C|z|^2\) for \(|z|\le1\).  For \(|z|\ge1\), positivity and
normalization of the translated Poisson density give
\(|J(z)|\le\|b_\Sigma\|_\infty\le
\|b_\Sigma\|_\infty|z|^2\).  Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-global-quadratic-pairing}
 |J(z)|\le C_{d,\Sigma}|z|^2,
 \qquad z\in\RR^d.
\end{equation}

Let \(B_2(y,z)\) denote the homogeneous degree-two Taylor term of
\(F_y(z)\) at zero.  Taylor expansion under the integral gives
\[
 J(z)=\int b_\Sigma(y)B_2(y,z)\,\Omega_d(\dd y)+o(|z|^2).
\]
The covariance-mode identity proved in
Theorem~\ref{thm:multidim-covariance-algebra} is
\(\EE B_2(y,X)=b_\Sigma(y)\).  Since \(\EE|X|^2<\infty\),
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-global-quadratic-pairing} and dominated
convergence imply
\[
 \EE[t^2J(X/t)]
 \longrightarrow
 \int b_\Sigma(y)\EE B_2(y,X)\,\Omega_d(\dd y)
 =\int b_\Sigma^2\dd\Omega_d.
\]
Because \(\int b_\Sigma\dd\Omega_d=0\), the left side is precisely the
left side of \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-weak-pairing}.

We also have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-l1-smallness}
 \|\delta_t\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Indeed, Minkowski's integral inequality bounds this norm by
\[
 \EE\left\|P_1^{(d)}(\mathord\cdot-X/t)-P_1^{(d)}
 \right\|_{L^1(\RR^d)},
\]
which tends to zero by translation continuity and dominated convergence.

Put \(r_t=\delta_t-u_t\).  Equations
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-weak-pairing} and
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-l1-smallness} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-remainder-pairing}
 t^2\int b_\Sigma r_t\dd\Omega_d\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad \|r_t\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
Uniformly in \(y\),
\[
 \log(1+u_t)=u_t+O(t^{-4}).
\]
It follows from \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-remainder-pairing} that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-remainder}
 \int r_t\log(1+u_t)\dd\Omega_d=o(t^{-4}).
\end{equation}
On the other hand, with
\(\Phi(s)=(1+s)\log(1+s)-s\), boundedness of \(b_\Sigma\) gives
\begin{align}
 \int(1+u_t)\log(1+u_t)\dd\Omega_d
 &=\int\Phi(u_t)\dd\Omega_d \notag\\
 &=\frac{t^{-4}}2\int b_\Sigma^2\dd\Omega_d+O(t^{-6})
 =t^{-4}\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)+O(t^{-6}).
 \label{eq:covariance-proxy-self-cross-entropy}
\end{align}
Combining \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-remainder} and
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-self-cross-entropy} yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-entropy-asymptotic}
 \int(1+\delta_t)\log(1+u_t)\dd\Omega_d
 =t^{-4}\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)+o(t^{-4}).
\end{equation}

Finally, the exact logarithmic identity is
\[
 H_d(t)=D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|k_{\Sigma,t})
 +\int(1+\delta_t)\log(1+u_t)\dd\Omega_d.
\]
 All terms are finite for large \(t\).  Indeed, convexity of relative entropy
 and
 \[
  D_{\rm KL}\!\left(P_1^{(d)}(\mathord\cdot-z)\,\middle\|\,
  P_1^{(d)}\right)
  \le \frac{d+1}{2}\log\{2(1+|z|^2)\}
  \le C_d(1+|z|^2)
 \]
 bound \(H_d(t)\) by an integrable average under
 \(\EE|X|^2<\infty\).  The first inequality follows from
 \(1+|w+z|^2\le2(1+|w|^2)(1+|z|^2)\).  Moreover,
 \(\log(1+u_t)\) is bounded.  Substitution of
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-cross-entropy-asymptotic} proves
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}.  Nonnegativity of relative
entropy then gives \eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-equivalence}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Raw-tail Poisson-energy decomposition]
\label{thm:raw-tail-poisson-energy-decomposition}
Let \(d\ge1\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR^d\) with
mean \(\bar\gamma\) and finite covariance matrix \(\Sigma\).  Write
\(\nu_c\) for its centred law, put \(a=(d+1)/2\), and define
\[
 \Omega_d(\dd y)=P_1^{(d)}(y)\dd y,
 \qquad
 F_y(z)=\left(\frac{1+|y|^2}{1+|y-z|^2}\right)^a,
 \qquad
 \Phi(v)=(1+v)\log(1+v)-v.
\]
For \(t>0\), let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-poisson-potential}
 V_t^\nu(y):=\int_{\{|x|>t\}}F_y(x/t)\,\nu_c(\dd x).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-entropy-decomposition}
 H_d(t)=\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)t^{-4}
 +\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d+o(t^{-4}).
\end{equation}
Consequently, the covariance-proxy defect in
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-functional} satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-proxy-decomposition}
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)
 =t^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d+o(1),
\end{equation}
and hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-proxy-equivalence}
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 t^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
No regular-variation or absolute-continuity hypothesis is required.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Translation permits us to assume that \(\nu\) is centred.  Set
\[
 \delta_t(y)=\int\{F_y(x/t)-1\}\nu(\dd x),
 \qquad u_t=t^{-2}b_\Sigma,
 \qquad \tau_t=\nu(|x|>t).
\]
Finite second moment gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-mass-smallness}
 \tau_t\le t^{-2}\int_{|x|>t}|x|^2\nu(\dd x)=o(t^{-2}).
\end{equation}
Let \(\ell_y(z)\) and \(B_2(y,z)\) be the homogeneous degree-one and
degree-two Taylor terms of \(F_y(z)\) at zero.  Direct differentiation of
the rational function \(F_y\), together with translation continuity of
the first two derivatives of \(P_1^{(d)}\) in \(L^1(\RR^d)\), gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-local-taylor-modulus}
 \|F_\cdot(z)-1-\ell_\cdot(z)-B_2(\cdot,z)\|_\infty
 +\|F_\cdot(z)-1-\ell_\cdot(z)-B_2(\cdot,z)\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}
 \le \varepsilon_d(|z|)|z|^2
\end{equation}
for \(|z|\le1\), where \(\varepsilon_d\) is bounded on \([0,1]\) and
\(\varepsilon_d(r)\to0\) as \(r\downarrow0\).  The Taylor coefficients
themselves satisfy, for \(j=1,2\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-taylor-coefficient-norms}
 \|B_j(\cdot,z)\|_\infty+
 \|B_j(\cdot,z)\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}\le C_d|z|^j,
 \qquad B_1=\ell.
\end{equation}

Centering and the covariance-mode identity
\(\int B_2(y,x)\nu(\dd x)=b_\Sigma(y)\) now yield the exact split
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-quotient-split}
 \delta_t=u_t+V_t^\nu-\tau_t+R_t,
\end{equation}
where
\[
 R_t=
 \int_{|x|\le t}\{F_\cdot(x/t)-1-\ell_\cdot(x/t)
                   -B_2(\cdot,x/t)\}\nu(\dd x)
 -\int_{|x|>t}\{\ell_\cdot(x/t)+B_2(\cdot,x/t)\}\nu(\dd x).
\]
Equations \eqref{eq:raw-tail-local-taylor-modulus} and
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-taylor-coefficient-norms} imply
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-remainder-smallness}
 \|R_t\|_\infty+\|R_t\|_{L^1(\Omega_d)}=o(t^{-2}).
\end{equation}
Indeed, after multiplication by \(t^2\), the interior remainder is
dominated by \(C_d|x|^2\) and converges pointwise to zero.  The omitted
linear and quadratic tail terms are bounded respectively by
\[
 Ct^{-1}\int_{|x|>t}|x|\nu(\dd x)
 \le Ct^{-2}\int_{|x|>t}|x|^2\nu(\dd x)
 \quad\text{and}\quad
 Ct^{-2}\int_{|x|>t}|x|^2\nu(\dd x).
\]

The identity
\(F_y(z)\Omega_d(\dd y)=P_1^{(d)}(y-z)\dd y\) gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-potential-normalization}
 V_t^\nu\ge0,
 \qquad \int V_t^\nu\dd\Omega_d=\tau_t.
\end{equation}
We next justify the nonlinear separation.  If \(|u|\le1/4\) and \(v\ge0\),
two integrations of \(\Phi''(s)=(1+s)^{-1}\) over the rectangle joining
\((0,0)\) to \((u,v)\) give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-phi-interaction}
 |\Phi(u+v)-\Phi(u)-\Phi(v)|\le C|u|v.
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:raw-tail-mass-smallness} and
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-remainder-smallness}, all negative arguments in
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-quotient-split} are uniformly larger than \(-1/2\) for
large \(t\).  The mean-value theorem, the bound
\(\log(1+v)\le v\), and \eqref{eq:raw-tail-potential-normalization} then give
\begin{align}
 \int|\Phi(\delta_t)-
 \Phi(u_t+V_t^\nu-\tau_t)|\dd\Omega_d&=o(t^{-4}),
 \label{eq:raw-tail-remove-remainder}\\
 \int|\Phi(u_t+V_t^\nu-\tau_t)-
 \Phi(u_t+V_t^\nu)|\dd\Omega_d&=o(t^{-4}),
 \label{eq:raw-tail-remove-mass}\\
 \int|\Phi(u_t+V_t^\nu)-\Phi(u_t)-
 \Phi(V_t^\nu)|\dd\Omega_d&=o(t^{-4}).
 \label{eq:raw-tail-remove-interaction}
\end{align}
For clarity, the first left side is bounded by
\(C\|R_t\|_\infty\tau_t+C t^{-2}\|R_t\|_1
 +C\|R_t\|_\infty\|R_t\|_1\); the second is bounded by
\(C\tau_t(\tau_t+t^{-2})\); and the third is at most
\(C\|u_t\|_\infty\tau_t\).  These bounds are all \(o(t^{-4})\).

Finally, boundedness and zero mean of \(b_\Sigma\) imply
\[
 \int\Phi(u_t)\dd\Omega_d
 =\frac{t^{-4}}2\int b_\Sigma^2\dd\Omega_d+O(t^{-6})
 =\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)t^{-4}+O(t^{-6}).
\]
Since \(H_d(t)=\int\Phi(\delta_t)\dd\Omega_d\), equations
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-remove-remainder}--
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-remove-interaction} prove
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-entropy-decomposition}.  Comparing it with
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition} proves
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-proxy-decomposition} and the equivalence.
\end{proof}

For a formulation involving only masses of moving sets, define
\[
 A_{t,0}(y)=\{|x|>t:\ |x-ty|<t\},
\]
and, for \(k\ge1\),
\[
 A_{t,k}(y)=\{|x|>t:\ 2^{k-1}t\le|x-ty|<2^kt\}.
\]
Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:moving-annulus-tail-functional}
 M_t^\nu(y)=(1+|y|^2)^a\left\{
 \nu_c(A_{t,0}(y))+
 \sum_{k\ge1}2^{-k(d+1)}\nu_c(A_{t,k}(y))\right\}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:moving-annulus-tail-energy}
 G_\nu(t)=t^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(M_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d.
\end{equation}

\begin{corollary}[Moving-annulus raw-tail criterion]
\label{cor:moving-annulus-tail-criterion}
Under the hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{thm:raw-tail-poisson-energy-decomposition},
\[
 t^4H_d(t)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 G_\nu(t)\longrightarrow0.
\]
This criterion applies without change to absolutely continuous, singular,
and purely atomic laws.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
On \(A_{t,0}(y)\), the factor
\((1+|y-x/t|^2)^{-a}\) lies between \(2^{-a}\) and \(1\).  On
\(A_{t,k}(y)\), \(k\ge1\), it is comparable, with constants depending only
on \(d\), to \(2^{-k(d+1)}\).  Hence
\[
 c_dM_t^\nu(y)\le V_t^\nu(y)\le C_dM_t^\nu(y).
\]
For each fixed \(c>0\), the ratio \(\Phi(cv)/\Phi(v)\) has positive finite
limits \(c^2\) at zero and \(c\) at infinity and is continuous on
\((0,\infty)\).  Thus fixed multiplicative changes of the argument preserve
two-sided comparability of \(\Phi\).  The conclusion follows from
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-proxy-equivalence}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Thin-shell aggregation precedes the entropy nonlinearity]
\label{prop:pre-phi-thin-shell-aggregation}
Fix \(d\ge4\), and set
\[
 p=\frac{4(d+1)}{d+5},\qquad q=\frac{d+5}{d+1}.
\]
Let \(B_n\to\infty\), choose \(R_n\to\infty\), and put
\(a_n=B_nR_n^{-p}\to0\) and
\(t_n=\kappa R_na_n^{1/(d+1)}\), where \(\kappa>0\) is fixed and
sufficiently small.  Let \(K_n\) be integers such that
\[
 B_n^qK_n^{1-q}\longrightarrow0.
\]
There are atoms \(\eta_{n,j}=(a_n/K_n)\delta_{r_{n,j}e_1}\),
\(1\le j\le K_n\), with
\(R_n\le r_{n,j}\le R_n+t_n/10\), for which
\begin{align}
 \sum_{j=1}^{K_n}\sup_{t>0}t^4
 \int\Phi(V_t^{\eta_{n,j}})\dd\Omega_d&\longrightarrow0,
 \label{eq:thin-shell-separated-energy}\\
 t_n^4\int\Phi\!\left(\sum_{j=1}^{K_n}
 V_{t_n}^{\eta_{n,j}}\right)\dd\Omega_d&\longrightarrow+\infty.
 \label{eq:thin-shell-aggregated-energy}
\end{align}
Thus summing shellwise nonlinear energies cannot replace aggregation of the
positive tail potential before applying \(\Phi\).
Here \(V_t^\eta\) denotes the expression on the right side of
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-poisson-potential} with the finite measure \(\eta\) in
place of \(\nu_c\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The inequality \(\Phi(v)\le C_qv^q\), \(v\ge0\), and the exact translation
identity \eqref{eq:critical-lq-exact-translation-norm} show that an atom of
mass \(m\) at radius \(r\) satisfies
\[
 \sup_{t>0}t^4\int\Phi(V_t^{m\delta_{re_1}})\dd\Omega_d
 \le C_dr^4m^q.
\]
Indeed, the potential vanishes for \(t\ge r\); for \(t<r\), the right side
of \eqref{eq:critical-lq-exact-translation-norm} is bounded by
\(C_d\{1+(r/t)^4\}\).  Since \(r_{n,j}\asymp R_n\), summation gives
\[
 \sum_{j=1}^{K_n}\sup_{t>0}t^4
 \int\Phi(V_t^{\eta_{n,j}})\dd\Omega_d
 \le C_dR_n^4a_n^qK_n^{1-q}
 =C_dB_n^qK_n^{1-q},
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:thin-shell-separated-energy}.

At scale \(t_n\), all translated centres \(r_{n,j}e_1/t_n\) lie within
distance \(1/10\) of \(R_ne_1/t_n\).  On a fixed sufficiently small ball
about the latter point,
\[
 \sum_{j=1}^{K_n}V_{t_n}^{\eta_{n,j}}
 \ge c_da_n(R_n/t_n)^{d+1}=c_d\kappa^{-(d+1)}.
\]
The \(\Omega_d\)-measure of this ball is bounded below by
\(c_d(t_n/R_n)^{d+1}=c_d\kappa^{d+1}a_n\).  Therefore
\[
 t_n^4\int\Phi\!\left(\sum_jV_{t_n}^{\eta_{n,j}}\right)\dd\Omega_d
 \ge c_dt_n^4a_n
 =c_dR_n^4a_n^q=c_dB_n^q\longrightarrow\infty,
\]
after decreasing \(\kappa\), if necessary, so that the fixed lower value of
the potential is nonzero.  This proves
\eqref{eq:thin-shell-aggregated-energy}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Proxy characterization at the critical vague boundary]
\label{cor:critical-rv-proxy-characterization}
Fix \(d\ge4\), set
\[
 p_d=\frac{4(d+1)}{d+5},
 \qquad
 \mu_\sigma(\dd\rho,\dd\theta)
 =p_d\rho^{-p_d-1}\dd\rho\,\sigma(\dd\theta),
\]
and let \(\nu\) have mean \(\bar\gamma\), finite covariance \(\Sigma\), and
centred law \(\nu_c\).  Suppose that, for a slowly varying \(L\),
\[
 \frac{r^{p_d}}{L(r)}\nu_c(r\,\mathord\cdot)
 \xrightarrow[r\to\infty]{v}\mu_\sigma
 \quad\text{on }\RR^d\setminus\{0\},
 \qquad
 \ell_L(t):=\int_{R_0}^t\frac{L(s)}s\dd s\longrightarrow\infty.
\]
Then
\[
 t^4H_d(t)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0.
\]
In particular, for the two laws \(\nu_{\rm ac}\) and \(\nu_{\rm cl}\) in
Theorem~\ref{thm:critical-vague-tail-nonidentifiability},
\[
 \mathcal C_{\nu_{\rm ac},\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad
 \limsup_{t\to\infty}\mathcal C_{\nu_{\rm cl},\Sigma}(t)=+\infty.
\]
Thus the spectral measure and slowly varying factor do not determine the
covariance-proxy defect trajectory.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The equivalence is Theorem~\ref{thm:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition};
the regular-variation assumptions specify the critical class but are not
needed for that decomposition.  Its combination with
\eqref{eq:critical-vague-tail-contrasting-entropy} gives the two final
assertions.
\end{proof}

"""Minimal exact replay of the Laurent constant-term recurrence.

Run from the paper root:

    python certificates/laurent_recurrence_minicheck.py
    python certificates/laurent_recurrence_minicheck.py 2 6
    python certificates/laurent_recurrence_minicheck.py 2,3,3

This is a compact independent audit of the deterministic recurrence in the
manuscript: it builds the all-sign Laurent coefficients q_{n,k}, iterates the
finite dictionary recurrence for C_j(s), and returns exact constant terms for
arbitrary requested rows.  With no row argument it checks the rows used through
total order eight.  It uses only Python standard-library exact rational
arithmetic.
"""

import argparse
from collections import defaultdict
from fractions import Fraction as F
from math import comb

Z, ONE, I = (F(0), F(0)), (F(1), F(0)), (F(0), F(1))
ROWS = [
    (2, 2), (2, 3), (2, 4), (3, 3), (2, 2, 2),
    (2, 5), (3, 4), (2, 2, 3),
    (2, 6), (3, 5), (4, 4), (2, 2, 4), (2, 3, 3), (2, 2, 2, 2),
]
EXPECTED = {
    (2, 2): (F(1, 4), 0), (2, 3): Z, (2, 4): (-F(1, 8), 0),
    (3, 3): (F(3, 16), 0), (2, 2, 2): (-F(3, 32), 0),
    (2, 5): Z, (3, 4): Z, (2, 2, 3): Z,
    (2, 6): (F(3, 64), 0), (3, 5): (-F(15, 128), 0),
    (4, 4): (F(5, 32), 0), (2, 2, 4): (F(3, 32), 0),
    (2, 3, 3): (-F(9, 128), 0), (2, 2, 2, 2): (F(9, 64), 0),
}


def add(a, b):
    return a[0] + b[0], a[1] + b[1]


def mul(a, b):
    return a[0] * b[0] - a[1] * b[1], a[0] * b[1] + a[1] * b[0]


def scale(a, c):
    return a[0] * c, a[1] * c


def lam(n):
    if n % 2 == 0:
        return F((-1) ** (n // 2), 2**n), F(0)
    return scale((F(0), F(-1)), F((-1) ** ((n - 1) // 2), 2**n))


def eps(n, k):
    return 1 if n % 2 == 0 or k > 0 else -1


def q(n, k):
    return scale(lam(n), eps(n, k) * comb(n - 1, abs(k) - 1))


def constant_term(row):
    c = {0: ONE}
    for n in row:
        d = defaultdict(lambda: Z)
        for s, a in c.items():
            for k in list(range(-n, 0)) + list(range(1, n + 1)):
                d[s + k] = add(d[s + k], mul(a, q(n, k)))
        c = {s: a for s, a in d.items() if a != Z}
    return c.get(0, Z)


def integer_recurrence(row, weight):
    c = {0: 1}
    for n in row:
        d = defaultdict(int)
        for s, a in c.items():
            for k in list(range(-n, 0)) + list(range(1, n + 1)):
                d[s + k] += a * weight(n, k)
        c = {s: a for s, a in d.items() if a}
    return c.get(0, 0)


def fmt(z):
    re, im = z
    if im == 0:
        return str(re)
    if re == 0:
        return f"{im}i"
    sign = "+" if im > 0 else "-"
    return f"{re}{sign}{abs(im)}i"


def parse_rows(raw_rows):
    if not raw_rows:
        return []
    if len(raw_rows) == 1 and "," in raw_rows[0]:
        return [tuple(int(part) for part in raw_rows[0].split(",") if part)]
    return [tuple(int(part) for part in raw_rows)]


def print_row(row):
    value = constant_term(row)
    count = integer_recurrence(row, lambda n, k: 1)
    signed = integer_recurrence(row, lambda n, k: eps(n, k) * comb(n - 1, abs(k) - 1))
    print(f"{row!s:14s} |K|={count:2d} signed={signed:3d} J={fmt(value)}")


def main():
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(
        description="Exact rational Laurent constant-term recurrence."
    )
    parser.add_argument(
        "row",
        nargs="*",
        help="row as space-separated integers, or one comma-separated row such as 2,3,3",
    )
    args = parser.parse_args()
    requested = parse_rows(args.row)

    if requested:
        for row in requested:
            if not row or any(n <= 0 for n in row):
                raise SystemExit(f"invalid row: {row}")
            print_row(row)
        return

    for row in ROWS:
        value = constant_term(row)
        assert value == EXPECTED[row], f"{row}: got {value}, expected {EXPECTED[row]}"
        print_row(row)

    print("All Laurent recurrence rows through total order eight verified exactly.")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
